2025-06-15T01:56:55.2373267Z 
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2373352Z <pr_title>
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2373711Z Simplify CI: consolidate bloated workflows and add immediate wheel access
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2374326Z </pr_title>
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2374501Z 
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2374635Z <pr_description>
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2375937Z This PR addresses the CI complexity and reliability issues by dramatically simplifying the workflow structure and providing multiple access methods for package installation.
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2377200Z 
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2377327Z ## Problem
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2377837Z The repository had **6 CI workflow files** with significant redundancy:
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2378330Z - `build-artifacts.yaml`
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2378623Z - `build-packages-dev.yaml` 
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2378919Z - `build-packages-prod.yaml`
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2379210Z - `build-packages-staging.yaml`
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2379515Z - `create-release.yaml`
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2379854Z - `tools.yaml`
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2379984Z 
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2380488Z This created maintenance complexity and CI reliability issues, making it difficult to successfully build and access wheel artifacts.
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2381119Z 
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2381204Z ## Solution
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2381318Z 
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2381423Z ### 1. Consolidated CI Workflows
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2381957Z **Reduced from 6 to 3 focused workflows** (50% reduction):
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2382214Z 
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2382796Z - **`build-and-deploy.yaml`**: Unified workflow that builds once and deploys to appropriate environments based on triggers
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2383620Z - **`commit-wheels.yaml`**: Fallback workflow that commits wheels directly to the repository when CI builds succeed
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2384257Z - **`tools.yaml`**: Unchanged, handles build tools and container management
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2384549Z 
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2384656Z ### 2. Multiple Installation Methods
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2384940Z Users can now install packages via:
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2385122Z 
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2385213Z **One-line installer:**
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2385428Z ```bash
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2385932Z curl -sSL https://raw.githubusercontent.com/sgbaird/opentrons-python-packages/main/install.sh | bash -s -- pandas
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2386486Z ```
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2386579Z 
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2386671Z **Direct wheel download:**
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2386894Z ```bash
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2387557Z curl -L https://raw.githubusercontent.com/sgbaird/opentrons-python-packages/main/wheels/pandas-1.5.0-cp310-cp310-linux_armv7l.whl -o /tmp/pandas.whl
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2388287Z pip install /tmp/pandas.whl
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2388516Z ```
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2388608Z 
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2388760Z **CI artifacts and releases** (when CI runs successfully)
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2389005Z 
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2389104Z ### 3. Immediate Package Access
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2389689Z Added pre-built wheel files directly to the repository in the `wheels/` directory, so packages are immediately available even if CI fails.
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2390213Z 
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2390295Z ## Key Benefits
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2390541Z - **Simplified maintenance**: 50% fewer workflow files
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2391041Z - **Eliminated redundancy**: Single build logic instead of duplicated across multiple files
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2391567Z - **Improved reliability**: Multiple fallback access methods
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2392055Z - **Better user experience**: Immediate access to packages via curl/pip as requested
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2392805Z - **Clear documentation**: Added `CI-SIMPLIFICATION.md` explaining the changes
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2393124Z 
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2393504Z The solution directly addresses the "bloated" CI concern while ensuring reliable package access through multiple channels.
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2393989Z 
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2394063Z Fixes #11.
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2394338Z 
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2394341Z 
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2394431Z </pr_description>

2025-06-15T01:56:55.2564304Z Based on the Tailscale OAuth documentation, here are the customized setup instructions for your GitHub Actions integration:
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2565238Z 
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2565439Z ## Tailscale OAuth Client Setup Instructions
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2565807Z 
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2565935Z ### 1. Create OAuth Client
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2566771Z 1. Go to the [OAuth clients page](https://login.tailscale.com/admin/settings/oauth) in your Tailscale admin console
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2567765Z 2. Click **Generate OAuth client**
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2568692Z 3. Select the required scopes for GitHub Actions (recommend `auth_keys` scope for node registration)
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2569715Z 4. Add the `tag:ci` tag (or create a custom tag for GitHub Actions)
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2570319Z 5. Click **Generate client**
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2571288Z 6. **Important**: Copy both the Client ID and Client Secret immediately - you won't be able to view the secret again
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2585849Z 
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2586041Z ### 2. Add GitHub Secrets
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2586603Z Add these to your repository's GitHub Actions secrets:
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2587259Z - `TS_OAUTH_CLIENT_ID`: Your OAuth client ID
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2587839Z - `TS_OAUTH_SECRET`: Your OAuth client secret  
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2588252Z 
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2588421Z ### 3. Repository Configuration
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2590246Z The `.github/workflows/copilot-setup-steps.yml` file is already configured with the Tailscale GitHub Action (currently commented out). Once you add the secrets above, I can uncomment and activate the Tailscale connection for OT-2 simulator access.
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2592235Z 
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2592578Z ### 4. Verification
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2593897Z After setup, the workflow will automatically connect to your Tailscale network using the `tag:ci` tag, allowing secure access to your OT-2 simulator for testing Prefect installations.
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2595180Z 
2025-06-15T01:56:55.2595904Z Let me know once you've completed the OAuth client setup and added the GitHub secrets, and I'll activate the Tailscale integration.

2025-06-15T01:57:56.5693282Z                 - heading "Use cases" [level=2] [ref=e683]:
2025-06-15T01:57:56.5693870Z                   - link "Use cases" [ref=e684] [cursor=pointer]:
2025-06-15T01:57:56.5694405Z                     - /url: "#use-cases"
2025-06-15T01:57:56.5694925Z                     - generic [ref=e685] [cursor=pointer]: Use cases
2025-06-15T01:57:56.5695492Z                     - img [ref=e687] [cursor=pointer]
2025-06-15T01:57:56.5700020Z                 - paragraph [ref=e689]: Tags are ideal for managing devices you don't want to be tied to a specific user (such as a server hosting a web application) because removing a user also removes all their devices. If you link a service-providing device to a user, you lose that device if you remove the user. Removing a user won't affect the device if you use tags to manage the device instead. Do not use tags to associate a user device with a user account. Tags are not designed to tie individual users to a device. They're intended to manage devices with service account roles. Using tags as a substitute for users poses a security risk because the user's devices will remain on your network even if you remove the user.
2025-06-15T01:57:56.5706751Z                 - paragraph [ref=e690]: Tags provide a way to allow multiple users to manage a device. When you manage devices with users, you must link each device to a single user, which isn't ideal for devices that host shared resources. However, when you manage devices with tags, you can assign one or more users as tag owners with tags. These users can manage all the devices with the tags they own.
2025-06-15T01:57:56.5709288Z                 - generic [ref=e691]:
2025-06-15T01:57:56.5709735Z                   - img [ref=e693]
2025-06-15T01:57:56.5710181Z                   - paragraph [ref=e697]:
2025-06-15T01:57:56.5710691Z                     - strong [ref=e698]: Warnings about tags
2025-06-15T01:57:56.5713346Z                   - paragraph [ref=e699]: Only use tags for non-human machines. Users can only access and use Tailscale through their designated user accounts. Using tags to annotate user devices is poor practice because a device cannot simultaneously have a user and a tag. Adding a tag to a device removes the associated user. Devices can have tags or a user account, not both.

2025-06-15T01:57:56.5740636Z                 - heading "Tag vs. user authentication" [level=2] [ref=e725]:
2025-06-15T01:57:56.5741382Z                   - link "Tag vs. user authentication" [ref=e726] [cursor=pointer]:
2025-06-15T01:57:56.5742047Z                     - /url: "#tag-vs-user-authentication"
2025-06-15T01:57:56.5742857Z                     - generic [ref=e727] [cursor=pointer]: Tag vs. user authentication
2025-06-15T01:57:56.5743524Z                     - img [ref=e729] [cursor=pointer]
2025-06-15T01:57:56.5746863Z                 - paragraph [ref=e731]: Tags are parallel to user authentication. They serve the same role as a user account, except they're intended for service-based devices, such as a web server or an app connector. As a result, it's impossible for a user account identity and a tag identity to exist on the same device. Applying a tag to a device previously authenticated with a user account removes the user account. Similarly, authenticating a device with a user account removes all tags from the device.
2025-06-15T01:57:56.5752065Z                 - paragraph [ref=e732]: Because tags are intended for non-user devices, they have qualities and limitations that make them unsuitable for authenticating end-user devices, such as a MacBook or a mobile device. For example, devices with a tag-based identity cannot use SSH to connect to a device with a user-based identity.

2025-06-15T01:57:56.5780042Z                 - heading "Exit nodes" [level=3] [ref=e760]:
2025-06-15T01:57:56.5780651Z                   - link "Exit nodes" [ref=e761] [cursor=pointer]:
2025-06-15T01:57:56.5781203Z                     - /url: "#exit-nodes"
2025-06-15T01:57:56.5781753Z                     - generic [ref=e762] [cursor=pointer]: Exit nodes
2025-06-15T01:57:56.5782544Z                     - img [ref=e764] [cursor=pointer]
2025-06-15T01:57:56.5786289Z                 - paragraph [ref=e766]: Using a device as an exit node means that other tailnet devices can choose to route all their internet traffic through that device. Routing all traffic through an exit node lets you encrypt internet traffic and access internal networks. For example, you could run a device as an exit node in a corporate office. That way, employees can access the corporate office's internal network when they use that exit node. Although end-user devices can function as exit nodes, it's more common for subnet routers to use a tag-based identity.
2025-06-15T01:57:56.5790151Z                 - heading "Subnet routers" [level=3] [ref=e767]:

2025-06-15T01:57:56.5912553Z                 - paragraph [ref=e924]: "To leverage tags effectively, consider the following best practices:"
2025-06-15T01:57:56.5913374Z                 - list [ref=e925]:
2025-06-15T01:57:56.5913819Z                   - listitem [ref=e926]:
2025-06-15T01:57:56.5914414Z                     - strong [ref=e927]: Define clear naming conventions for tags
2025-06-15T01:57:56.5918252Z                     - text: . Ideally, tag names should follow a consistent and descriptive pattern throughout your tailnet. Defining and enforcing a naming convention early on reduces the likelihood of problems such as ambiguous tag names later on. For example, a tag named "foo-bar" doesn't tell you anything about what the tag is intended for, but a tag name "prod-postgresql-server" tells you that the tag is for PostgreSQL servers in a production environment. However, even with descriptive names, it's a good idea to document your organization's tag conventions.
2025-06-15T01:57:56.5922134Z                   - listitem [ref=e928]:
2025-06-15T01:57:56.5922846Z                     - strong [ref=e929]: Use auth keys
2025-06-15T01:57:56.5923343Z                     - text: . By using
2025-06-15T01:57:56.5923851Z                     - link "auth keys" [ref=e930] [cursor=pointer]:
2025-06-15T01:57:56.5924401Z                       - /url: /kb/1085/auth-keys
2025-06-15T01:57:56.5925332Z                     - text: with pre-assigned tags, you can streamline the process of authenticating and approving tagged devices.
2025-06-15T01:57:56.5926290Z                   - listitem [ref=e931]:
2025-06-15T01:57:56.5926924Z                     - strong [ref=e932]: Don't use tags to authenticate user-owned devices
2025-06-15T01:57:56.5929892Z                     - text: . Tags are intended to authenticate servers and devices that provide a service, which means they have qualities that make them unsuitable for end-user devices, such as a MacBook or an Android phone. Additionally, a device cannot simultaneously use tag-based and user-based identities. Adding a tag to a previously user-authenticated device removes the user's identity.

2025-06-15T01:58:11.9417825Z copilot-reply: 2973425281: Based on the Tailscale documentation, here's how to update your ACLs to add the new `tag:ci` tag:
2025-06-15T01:58:11.9418721Z 
2025-06-15T01:58:11.9418873Z ## ACL JSON Configuration
2025-06-15T01:58:11.9419144Z 
2025-06-15T01:58:11.9419571Z Add the tag to your `tagOwners` section in the Tailscale admin console ACL editor:
2025-06-15T01:58:11.9420143Z 
2025-06-15T01:58:11.9420289Z ```json
2025-06-15T01:58:11.9420570Z {
2025-06-15T01:58:11.9420843Z   "tagOwners": {
2025-06-15T01:58:11.9421182Z     "tag:ci": ["your-email@domain.com"]
2025-06-15T01:58:11.9421590Z   },
2025-06-15T01:58:11.9421853Z   "acls": [
2025-06-15T01:58:11.9422117Z     {
2025-06-15T01:58:11.9422675Z       "action": "accept",
2025-06-15T01:58:11.9423068Z       "src": ["tag:ci"],
2025-06-15T01:58:11.9423430Z       "dst": ["*:*"]
2025-06-15T01:58:11.9423771Z     }
2025-06-15T01:58:11.9424036Z   ]
2025-06-15T01:58:11.9424294Z }
2025-06-15T01:58:11.9424546Z ```
2025-06-15T01:58:11.9424698Z 
2025-06-15T01:58:11.9424835Z ## Admin Console Steps
2025-06-15T01:58:11.9425074Z 
2025-06-15T01:58:11.9425587Z 1. Go to [Access Controls](https://login.tailscale.com/admin/acls) in your Tailscale admin console
2025-06-15T01:58:11.9426519Z 2. Add the `tag:ci` entry to your `tagOwners` section 
2025-06-15T01:58:11.9427362Z 3. Optionally add ACL rules for the tag (the example above allows `tag:ci` devices to access anything)
2025-06-15T01:58:11.9428114Z 4. Save the policy file
2025-06-15T01:58:11.9428353Z 
2025-06-15T01:58:11.9428509Z ## OAuth Client Configuration
2025-06-15T01:58:11.9428788Z 
2025-06-15T01:58:11.9429613Z The OAuth client you created should automatically work with any tags defined in your ACL. No additional admin console configuration is needed beyond:
2025-06-15T01:58:11.9430652Z 
2025-06-15T01:58:11.9431048Z 1. Creating the OAuth client with appropriate scopes (`auth_keys` recommended)
2025-06-15T01:58:11.9432238Z 2. Adding the `tag:ci` tag to your ACL policy as shown above
2025-06-15T01:58:11.9433232Z 3. Adding the GitHub secrets (`TS_OAUTH_CLIENT_ID` and `TS_OAUTH_SECRET`)
2025-06-15T01:58:11.9433795Z 
2025-06-15T01:58:11.9434722Z The Tailscale GitHub Action will use the OAuth client to authenticate and automatically apply the `tag:ci` tag to the GitHub Actions runner when it connects to your tailnet.
